A decision aid approach for sports performance evaluation and player activity tracking using fermatean fuzzy Z-information
Authors: Qiaoli Wei, Junwei Yao, Weitao Zheng
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers introduce a new decision-making framework which demonstrates greater capacity to handle uncertainty than traditional fuzzy set theory. The research study introduces a set of operational laws together with neutral aggregation operators which function as tools for handling Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers. The operators function as essential tools for multi-criteria decision-making because they enable assessment of complex systems which need simultaneous evaluation of multiple criteria. The research establishes new operational laws which extend the use of Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers to situations which demand flexible decision-making systems. The study investigates how to use Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers for assessing soccer player performance. The research investigates how well FF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document}N Neutrality Aggregation Operators that evaluate goal scoring, assists, tackles, and passing accuracy can provide accurate performance rankings. The aggregation process uses expert evaluations which assess both positive and negative contributions to create an unbiased football player ranking system. The case study demonstrates how Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers can be used to make actual decisions. The process compares rankings produced by FF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} Neutrality Aggregation Operators with WASPAS rankings to demonstrate how Fermatean fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers help solve complex decision-making situations which need to manage uncertainty and multiple factors for optimal outcomes.

Introduction

The process of player evaluation functions as a critical element for professional soccer teams to choose their players and develop effective training programs and establish their game strategies. Soccer player evaluations typically involve expert evaluations which contain subjective elements because experts assess players based on their personal judgment. The aforementioned characteristics will result in differences between two methods of ranking and the accuracy of their respective rankings. The dissertation resolves existing difficulties by developing a decision-making system through Machine Learning (ML) which uses an objective data-driven method to rank soccer players while preventing common assessment mistakes. The primary aim of this study is to rate soccer players based on key performance indicators (KPIs) gleaned partly from match statistics as well as from wearable sensor data. The KPIs of the study consist of goals scored assists passing accuracy and defensive contributions. These performance metrics stand for various features that are pertinent to judging somebody in a holistic way. The evaluation of all such patient attributes tied together satisfies the basic tenets for Multi-Attribute Group Decision Making (MAGDM), which is the simultaneous assessment and trade-off of numerous decision criteria for a holistic assessment of all the players’ contributions toward the team. That has allowed an accurate and fair judgment based on strengths and weaknesses across various dimensions of performance.

Review of literatures

Zadeh’s1 presentation of fuzzy set theory researchers have maintained its crucial role in developing uncertainty modeling methods. This field has experienced important breakthroughs according to several modern research studies. Versaci and La Foresta2 suggested a fuzzy-based method for the control of renewable energy flows in DC micro grids with composite photovoltaic wind turbine (PV-WT) generators and an energy storage system. Lopez et al.3 presented a calibrated Fuzzy Best-Worst Method to improve supply chain resilience in the COVID-19 pandemic. A new fuzzy logic-based machine learning technique was presented by Leung et al.4 to enhance predictive analytics in large transportation data. An expanded fuzzy framework which enables dynamic risk management5 in project environments.An established fuzzy optimization tools specifically for urban infrastructure6 development which demonstrated outstanding success in planning major-scale projects. The disaster management benefits from using fuzzy methods7 because these methods allow modeling uncertainty to make response mechanisms faster and more reliable. A decision support system for engineering design was developed by Hung et al.8 using an advanced fuzzy multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) methodology. The theory of fuzzy sets gained Intuitionistic Fuzzy Set (IFS) through the work of Atanassov9 which introduced non-membership and membership grades. According to Ali et al.10 three way decision model using IFS performed better than traditional diagnostic tools as an analytic instrument for health care settings. IFS serves agricultural planning functions through cheng and chen11 research by giving farmers dependable decision making capabilities when facing unreliable weather situations. IFS shows promise for improving e-commerce recommendation systems12 by handling uncertainties related to user preferences. Sugumar et al. illustrate how IFS contributes to renewable energy site13 choice to enable decisions that respect environmental justice criteria. Interval Valued IFS-based decision-making framework for MCDM14 that manage conflicting objectives. IFS to the financial risk analysis framework15 with a focus on coping with vagaries of the market.

The formation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\grave{Z}$$\end{document} numbers and IFS results in IF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\grave{Z}$$\end{document}-number16 (IF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\grave{Z}$$\end{document}N) which enhances information evaluation by giving more tools to uncertainty and reliability modeling. In this regard, practical applications of IF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\grave{Z}$$\end{document}N were made in supplier selection17 operations that improve evaluation methods when uncertain conditions rule the environment. A defuzzification of IF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\grave{Z}$$\end{document}N18 based fault diagnosis framework of enabled engineering systems to achieve higher accuracy in identifying process-related problems. AHP and TOPSIS19 approach for IF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document}N to develop an application on hydrogen storage technology selection.

Pythagorean fuzzy sets (PFS) introduced by Yager20 afford considerable flexibility in representing uncertainty through the relaxation of conditions imposed on the sum of membership and non-membership degrees. The recent literature stresses their applications in PFS across many fields. Karasan et al.21 applied PFS to environmental risk assessment, presenting better evaluation metrics. Some distance measure22 considered for PFS and their applications in pattern recognition. Under uncertain conditions the effective applications of PFS in intelligent traffic optimization23 of transportation systems. Li et al. (2021) applied PFS for image processing work resulting in improved edge detection efficiency. PFS Dombi aggregation24 operators and their application in decision support system PF Z-numbers (PF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document}Ns), as an integration of Pythagorean fuzzy sets and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} numbers, new decision-support tool for choosing green suppliers25 that uses an expanded EDAS methodology. Sari26 used PFZN models as a framework to handle energy management project which enhanced decision-making reliability. The use of PFZNs in Complex decision aid system and einstein aggregation operator in the PFZNs environment. A PFZN based neutrality aggregation model for AI-enabled energy efficiency management was proposed by Abbasi et al.27. This model shows that advanced fuzzy decision-making techniques can improve energy management by successfully managing imprecision and uncertainty in AI-driven systems. Fermatean fuzzy sets (FFS), proposed by Senapati and Yager28, extend Pythagorean fuzzy sets, where the sum of cube of membership and non-membership degrees is less than or equal to one, making it more flexible in dealing with uncertainty. While demonstrating how its application in intelligent systems frameworks offers a new methodology for evaluating decision-making uncertainty in a variety of circumstances, authors Deng and Wang29 developed a novel distance measure for Fermatean fuzzy sets. Fermatean fuzzy sets are the foundational functionality advantages of the alternative decision-making system created by Chakraborty et al.30 in the Spectrum of Engineering and Management Sciences. The correlation coefficients31 of Fermatean fuzzy sets for use in medical decision-making. Fermatean fuzzy sets offer effective applications for uncertain healthcare systems. Fermatean fuzzy logic sets in conjunction with the WASPAS32 methodology and a smart tool to evaluate green building suppliers. This demonstrates how Fermatean fuzzy sets. Recent developments in multi-criteria decision-making under fuzzy and uncertain environments have motivated the use of advanced aggregation operators. Several recent studies have contributed to this direction. Ali proposed a decision analysis framework using SWARA and Aczel-Alsina aggregation for (m, n)-cubic quasi-rung orthopair fuzzy sets33, demonstrating improved weighting and aggregation performance in complex decision environments. Similarly, Ali et al. developed prioritized Aczel-Alsina34 aggregation operators under p,q-quasirung orthopair fuzzy environments and applied them to sustainable supplier selection problems in the new energy vehicle industry, showing enhanced robustness under uncertainty. In another study, Ali and Popa introduced a Dombi power aggregation-based decision framework for smart city initiative prioritization under t-arbicular fuzzy environments35, providing flexible aggregation behavior for urban decision problems. Furthermore, Ali et al.36 applied the TODIM decision-making method with unknown weights under a t-arbicular fuzzy environment for optimal security system selection, highlighting the effectiveness of behavioral decision modeling under uncertainty. In addition, Azeem et al.37 proposed complex Fermatean fuzzy partitioned Maclaurin symmetric mean operators and applied them to real-world location selection problems, demonstrating strong aggregation capability in complex fuzzy decision environments.

These recent studies collectively demonstrate the growing importance of advanced fuzzy aggregation operators in handling uncertainty, reliability, and complex decision-making scenarios.

Motivations

Using Fermatean Fuzzy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document} Numbers38 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}), a complex mathematical model that combines FFS and Z-number characteristics, improves the precision of decision-making. FFSs are suitable for processing advanced levels of reluctance and uncertainty because of their flexibility and enhanced membership representation capabilities, which outperform those of intuitionistic and Pythagorean fuzzy sets. Little research has been done on aggregation operators that explicitly target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s. In order to improve expert opinion aggregation in situations involving uncertain decision-making, the current study applies a Neutrality Aggregation Operator for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} systems. The suggested method’s efficacy is compared and evaluated using the Weighted Aggregated Sum Product Assessment (WASPAS) technique. The Weighted Sum Model (WSM) and Weighted Product Model (WPM) are combined in the WASPAS technique for Multi-Attribute Decision Making (MADM).The complicated decision environments with expert opinions that either contradict one another or are ambiguous or uncommitted gave rise to this study. In real-world decision-making processes, fuzzy systems and Z-number-based aggregation techniques are insufficiently capable of representing impartial decisions.The current research on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s does not offer a systematic framework for combining expert opinions without sacrificing their neutral qualities. These models’ practical use in real-world applications is limited by their lack of adequate comparison frameworks. By enabling comparison to the well-established MADM approach WASPAS, the addition of a Neutrality Aggregation Operator to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s will close research gaps and aid in the development of more effective decision-making techniques.

Section "Analysis for Comparison" analyzes the results through comparison of the proposed method against the WASPAS approach to demonstrate its effectiveness. Section "Sensitivity Analysis with Respect to Weight Vector" of this article wraps up by conclusion and suggesting directions for future research. Preliminaries

This section establishes fundamental definitions together with operations and features of FFS and Z-numbers as well as their combinational form known as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} for baseline understanding of this research. An extension of IF and PF sets, the FFS provides greater ease of modeling uncertainty by allowing the sum of squares of membership and non-membership degrees: at most three (rather than one or two). This extended range shall better handle the vagueness and imprecise information related to decision-making problems. Z-numbers, on the other hand, hold a two-component configuration wherein the first component denotes uncertain information and the second designates its reliability.

Definition 2.1

1 Under the Entire Set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda$$\end{document}, the fuzzy set identified is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\wp }=\big \{(\chi ,\varpi _{{\wp }}(\nu )|\chi \in \Lambda )\big \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _{{\mu }}(\nu )$$\end{document} degrees of contentment,of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varsigma$$\end{document} in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Lambda }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _{{\Lambda }}:\Lambda \rightarrow [0,1].$$\end{document}

Definition 2.5

To compare \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\ell _i}= \Big \{({\varpi }_{i},\tau _{{\varpi }_{i}}),(\mu _{i},\tau _{\mu _{i}})\Big \}$$\end{document} we introduced the score function as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Im (\zeta _{\ell _i})=\frac{1+({\varpi }_{i},\tau _{{\varpi }_{i}})-(\mu _{i},\tau _{\mu _{i}})}{2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Im (\zeta _{\ell _i})\in [-1,1]$$\end{document}

Neutrality operators based on fermatean fuzzy Z- numbers

The effective analysis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s in decision-making requires establishing central mathematical operations. The fundamental operations of addition and scalar power and scalar multiplication with multiplications form a foundation for working with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s through their transformation and fusion process that maintains fuzzy and probabilistic properties. These operations create the base for developing aggregate methods including the Neutrality Operator while allowing decision systems to integrate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s. These operations receive formal definitions along with mathematical expressions through the subsequent subsections.

Neutrality aggregation operators of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}

The aggregation operators function as essential components of MAGDM to combine expert opinions with uncertainty and imprecision for producing unified decisions. When working with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} the aggregation process requires advanced handling because these numbers contain dual elements consisting of uncertain data with reliability measures. The proposed Neutrality Aggregation Operator serves to unify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s by executing an aggregation while upholding neutrality of expert evaluations. The proposed operator performs an efficient balance between contradictory or unsure expert evaluations to produce better informed decisions. The next part details the mathematical presentation and essential characteristics alongside practical examples of the proposed aggregation operator.

Theorem 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2,3...n$$\end{document} then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWA_{NA}$$\end{document} is Identified as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} FF\ddot{Z}NWA_{NA}(\zeta _{\ell _1},\zeta _{\ell _2},\zeta _{\ell 3} {\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}(\varpi _{i})^3}{\sum _{i=1}^{n}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}((\varpi _{i})^3+(\mu _{i})^3)}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{n}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of Proof

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}, we will use the mathematical induction approach to prove it using operations (1) and (3).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{c} \ddot{\Omega }_{1}\odot \zeta _{\ell _1}= \left\{ \begin{array}{c} \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}(\varpi _{1})^3}{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}((\varpi _{1})^3+(\mu _{1})^3)}(1-((\Theta _{1})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\ddot{\Omega _{1}}(\tau _{\varpi _{1}})^3}{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}((\tau _{\varpi _{1}})^3+(\tau _{\mu _{1}})^3)}(1-((\theta _{1})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}})}\Big )\\ \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}(\mu _{1})^3}{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}((\varpi _{1})^3+(\mu _{1})^3)}(1-((\Theta _{1})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}(\tau _{\mu _{1}})^3}{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}((\tau _{\varpi _{1}})^3+(\tau _{\mu _{1}})^3)}(1-((\theta _{1})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega }_{1}})}\Big )\\ \end{array} \right\} . \end{array}$$\end{document}

Example 4.3

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1=\{(0.25,0.39),(0.45,0.57)\}, \quad A_2=\{(0.50,0.39),(0.29,0.37)\}$$\end{document}be two Fermatean Fuzzy Z-Numbers with corresponding weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _1=0.2, \quad \Omega _2=0.8,$$\end{document}such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _1+\Omega _2=1$$\end{document}.

Using the proposed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FFZNWA_{NA}$$\end{document} operator, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{c} ~~ FFZNWA_{NA}(A_1,A_2)=\left\{ \begin{array}{c} \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}(\varpi _{i})^3}{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}((\varpi _{i})^3+(\mu _{i})^3)}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}(\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3}{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3+(\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}(\mu _{i})^3}{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}((\varpi _{i})^3+(\mu _{i})^3)}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}(\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3}{\sum _{i=1}^{2}{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3+(\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \end{array} \right\} . \end{array}$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _{i}=\root 3 \of {1-({\varpi _{i}})^{3}-({\mu _{i}})^{3}}$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{i}=\root 3 \of {1-(\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^{3}-(\tau _{\mu _{i}})^{3}}$$\end{document}

Substituting numerical values,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _{1}=\root 3 \of {1-(0.25)^{3}-(0.45)^{3}}=0.96$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{1}=\root 3 \of {1-(0.39)^{3}-(0.57)^{3}}=0.91$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _{2}=\root 3 \of {1-(0.50)^{3}-(0.29)^{3}}=0.95$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{1}=\root 3 \of {1-(0.39)^{3}-(0.37)^{3}}=0.96$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$= \left\{ \begin{aligned}&\root 3 \of { \frac{ 0.2(0.25^3)+0.8(0.50^3) }{ 0.2(0.25^3+0.45^3)+ 0.8(0.50^3+0.29^3) } \left( 1-(0.96^3)^{0.2}(0.95^3)^{0.8}\right) } \\&\root 3 \of { \frac{ 0.2(0.39^3)+0.8(0.39^3) }{ 0.2(0.39^3+0.57^3)+ 0.8(0.39^3+0.37^3) } \left( 1-(0.91^3)^{0.2}(0.96^3)^{0.8}\right) } \\&\root 3 \of { \frac{ 0.2(0.45^3)+0.8(0.29^3) }{ 0.2(0.25^3+0.45^3)+ 0.8(0.50^3+0.29^3) } \left( 1-(0.96^3)^{0.2}(0.95^3)^{0.8}\right) }\\&\root 3 \of { \frac{ 0.2(0.57^3)+0.8(0.37^3) }{ 0.2(0.39^3+0.57^3)+ 0.8(0.39^3+0.37^3) } \left( 1-(0.91^3)^{0.2}(0.96^3)^{0.8}\right) } \\ \end{aligned} \right\} .$$\end{document}After simplification,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=\{(0.47, 0.40),(0.36, 0.46)\}$$\end{document}

Monotonicity: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\ell _i}= \Big \{({\varpi }_{i},\tau _{{\varpi }_{i}}),(\mu _{i},\tau _{\mu _{i}})\Big \}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\ell _i}\subseteq \zeta _{\ell _i'}$$\end{document} then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta }((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta }((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{\beta }((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{\beta }((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \end{array} \right\} . \end{array}$$\end{document}

then we prove it for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=\beta +1$$\end{document} such as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} FF\ddot{Z}NWG_{NA}(\zeta _{\ell _1},\zeta _{\ell _2},\zeta _{\ell 3} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i=1}^{\beta }\ddot{\Omega _i}\zeta _{\ell _{i}}\bigoplus \ddot{\Omega _{\beta +1}}\zeta _{\ell _{\beta +1}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{c} ~~ =\left\{ \begin{array}{c} \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{\beta +1}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega \end{array}$$\end{document}

This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta +1$$\end{document} holds. Hence it is true for all n and it completes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Example 4.6

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1=\{(0.25,0.39),(0.45,0.57)\}, \quad A_2=\{(0.50,0.39),(0.29,0.37)\}$$\end{document}be two Fermatean Fuzzy Z-Numbers with corresponding weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _1=0.2, \quad \Omega _2=0.8,$$\end{document}such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _1+\Omega _2=1$$\end{document}.

Using the proposed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FFZNWG_{NA}$$\end{document} operator, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{c} ~~ FFZNWG_{NA}(A_1,A_2)=\left\{ \begin{array}{c} \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \Big (\root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\varpi _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\mu _{i})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\Theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})},\\ \root 3 \of {\frac{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}{\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\varpi _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}+\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\tau _{\mu _{i}})^3)^{{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}}}}(1-\prod _{i=1}^{2}((\theta _{i})^3)^{\ddot{\Omega _{i}}})}\Big )\\ \end{array} \right\} . \end{array}$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N_{NA}$$\end{document} operator for multi attributes group decision making

In this section, we have developed an algorithm that uses the suggested average and geometric aggregation operators to solve the Multiple Attributes Decision Making (MAGDM) problem. We also presented an example of MAGDM to demonstrate how these operators are used. Assume that we have a set of attributes represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\aleph =\{{\aleph _{1},\aleph _{2},\aleph _{3},... \aleph _{n}}\}$$\end{document}, and a set of alternatives represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\{{\Psi _{1},\Psi _{2},\Psi _{3},...\Psi _{n}}\}$$\end{document}. Each of these attributes has its own weight vectors, which are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\{\omega _1,\omega _2,....\omega _n\}$$\end{document}. To provide a proper weighting scheme, the weight vectors must meet the requirements that the weights belong to a closed unit interval (i.e., ranging from 0 to 1) and that their total equals 1. Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\ell }^{k}=[\zeta _{\ell _{ij}}^{k}]_{m\times n}$$\end{document} represents the FF\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document}N decision matrix.AlgorithmFig. 1Flow Chart of Algorithm.

Case study: soccer player performance evaluation

To demonstrate the applicability of the proposed aggregation operators under uncertainty, a case study involving soccer player performance evaluation is considered. Four alternatives are examined: Goalkeeper, Forward, Midfielder, and Defender, evaluated across multiple performance criteria. The sports industry of soccer continues to transform its performance assessment methods from traditional subjective measures into data-based statistical measures. Human judgment stands alone as an improper evaluation method because it reveals multiple errors through biased and inconsistent behavior. Traditional scouting reports which used mostly human evaluation techniques now get augmented through modern quantitative data collected from performance tracking technology systems. Modern soccer squads39 use big data metrics that include assessment of player movement distances together with heat maps and possession time analytic to determine the players’ contribution to team performance. These advanced metrics ensure objective and transparent analysis about team selection and in-game tactical changes and they also help with youth player development programs. GPS trackers40 combined with heart rate monitors and interferometers enabled coaches and their analysts to track real-time movements and biomechanics and physiological data for players through wearable devices. To understand performance levels better than simple goal statistics this measurement approach gives teams critical insights about player energetic steps and agility alongside fatigue monitoring metrics. Through their constant data collection methods teams build performance trend assessments that enable them to implement evidence-based adjustments during matches and training periods. Extensive performance data including positioning information, critical decision-making analysis and defensive and offensive roles emerge from the combination of computer vision algorithms with video analysis software.

Machine learning techniques, especially supervised learning algorithms, are leading the way in turning raw player data into useful insights. These algorithms can analyze historical match data41 to forecast player performance in future games, enabling coaches to make more informed decisions about game strategy and player usage. Supervised learning algorithms within machine learning operate as leading forces to generate valuable information from raw player data. Supervised learning algorithms apply historical match data to anticipate how players will perform in future games thus helping coaches create better decisions about team tactics and player usage. The model evaluates player match performance by analyzing their historical statistics and health status with active in-game metrics. Teams benefit from predictive models because these tools allow them to develop optimal strategies and enhance player performance while minimizing damage to players through strategic workforce management. Advanced machine learning systems enable the detection of previously unseen connections among sprint performance and attacking success and stamina behavior in match conclusion. The soccer performance evaluation system utilizes machine learning for tactical decisions and becomes essential in identifying prospective players through scouting as well as their developmental needs. By examining players from various teams and leagues, scouting departments can utilize machine learning models to pinpoint under performing players with potential for growth, as well as standout performers who could become future stars. These models are also valuable for tracking the progress of young players, assisting academies in monitoring their development over time and making informed choices about their future roles in the senior team. Through pattern recognition, machine learning can identify subtle performance-related details that might be overlooked by human analysts, making it an essential tool for player recruitment and talent development.

As soccer evolves further towards a more data-influenced sporting entity, the tremendous potential that machine learning and data analysis possess-to fundamentally transform the landscape of player performance assessment and hence, the decision-making processes of teams-is exponentially enhanced. By machine-learning-driven assessments of the players’ performance against facts, teams can better develop decision-making processes that become more dynamic and provide feedback via better player development or enhance the understanding of on-the-field success. In this case study, we focus on how machine learning algorithms can analyze key performance metrics-from passing accuracy to sprint speed-comprehensively, thereby giving soccer coaches actionable evaluations of their players’ on-field performances. The case study aims to provide a bridge between traditional performance metrics and modern data science; hence an assessment of contributions that integrates its distinctiveness, efficiency, and objectivity. The case study aims to provide ranks of soccer players according to KPI- key performance indicators-from match statistics and wearable sensor data. The uncertainty in skill assessment is treated through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}s, allowing for a more holistic descriptive assessment. Input data are provided by a team of soccer analysts and coaches, and then processed using the proposed aggregation technique.

Four soccer players from different positions were chosen to carry out performance appraisal with the aim of assessing their contribution towards the game. In this study, the analysis was limited to the following players:

Goalkeeper\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi _{1})$$\end{document}: The last line of defense, a goalkeeper group must be characterized by skills in shot-stopping, reflexes, and command of the penalty box. Some of the parameters employed to reflect the performance are saved, base clean-outs, good receptions, and making decisions under pressure.

Forward\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi _{2})$$\end{document}: A forward is a player capable of converting into goals the many potential scoring opportunities afforded by their mid fielders. Although the performance of the forward can be measured using goals scored, successful dribbles completed, and the number of chances for the attack created, other characteristics contributing to success are speed and finishing ability, alongside the ability to take up good positions.

Mid fielder\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi _{3})$$\end{document}: The link that might conceivably be said to rest between defense and attack, the mid fielder is responsible for maintaining possession, circulating passes, and creating goals coring opportunities. Since the mid fielder sets the tempo of the game, he/she must possess good passing accuracy, stamina, and spatial awareness.

Defender\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi _{4})$$\end{document}: A player generally assigned the task of protecting the goal. Skills needed include tackling, interceptions, and aerial duels. Performance figures calling for a defender must be taken from rates of blocking against attacks and distances cleared of dangerous balls from the defensive area.

The assessment of all the players will be according to the following four parameters.

Passing Accuracy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\aleph _{1})$$\end{document}: This is a measurement of the percentage of successful passes made by a player with regard to people who already have a bright future. It is highly important for midfielders and defenders because a good, well-placed pass means possession is retained and the transition is possible. It shows high decision-making and technical ability.

Sprint Speed\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\aleph _{2})$$\end{document}: Maximum sprint speed attained by a player during an active match. Speed really matters for forwards and wingers as it helps them get in front of defenders, find space for fast runs, and score goals.

Stamina (Minutes Maintained in High Performance)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\aleph _{3})$$\end{document}: This determines the capacity of a player to sustain effectiveness during a match. A little more stamina allows for extra effectiveness toward the end of the match to steal the ball and make another victory possible for the team.

Defensive Interceptions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\aleph _{4})$$\end{document}:This count includes the number of attempts made by the player to thwart the opponent’s attack through actual defensive plays successfully. A high interception count is vital for a defender because it denies scoring chances and allows for a much more stable team altogether.

In this scenario, we have a collection of alternatives denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\{{\Psi _{1},\Psi _{2},\Psi _{3},\Psi _{4}}\}$$\end{document},. We also have a set of attributes denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\aleph =\{{\aleph _{1},\aleph _{2},\aleph _{3},\aleph _{4}}\}$$\end{document}. To ensure the credibility and practical validity of the decision data, a panel of three domain experts was consulted for evaluation. The experts were selected based on their professional experience in soccer performance assessment and analytics.Expert-1 A certified professional coach with over 12 years of experience in tactical planning and player performance evaluation.Expert-2 A sports performance analyst with 8 years of experience in match analytics and data-driven player assessment.Expert-3 A former professional soccer player with 10 years of competitive experience and 5 years of experience in scouting and technical evaluation.

Expert-1 A certified professional coach with over 12 years of experience in tactical planning and player performance evaluation.

Expert-1 A certified professional coach with over 12 years of experience in tactical planning and player performance evaluation.

Expert-2 A sports performance analyst with 8 years of experience in match analytics and data-driven player assessment.

Expert-2 A sports performance analyst with 8 years of experience in match analytics and data-driven player assessment.

Expert-3 A former professional soccer player with 10 years of competitive experience and 5 years of experience in scouting and technical evaluation.

Expert-3 A former professional soccer player with 10 years of competitive experience and 5 years of experience in scouting and technical evaluation.

Each expert independently evaluated the four alternatives (Goalkeeper, Forward, Midfielder, and Defender) with respect to the defined criteria using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} assessments. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{Z}$$\end{document}-information component was used to represent the reliability degree associated with each evaluation. The importance weights of the criteria were determined based on expert judgment rather than arbitrary assignment. Each expert provided a weight vector reflecting the relative importance of the evaluation criteria. These individual weight vectors were aggregated using a normalized averaging approach to obtain the final decision weight vector. We have experts weight vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ddot{\Omega }=\{0.47,0.38,0.15\}$$\end{document} and attribute weight vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda =\{0.28, 0.25, 0.36, 0.11\}$$\end{document}, corresponding to the attributes. The decision evaluation is conducted within the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} framework. Weight vectors are perturbed to examine robustness, and aggregation is conducted using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWA_{NA}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWG_{NA}$$\end{document} operators. Additionally, a classical baseline method (WASPAS) is included for comparative purposes. The analysis investigates both ranking stability and statistical consistency under weight perturbations to validate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed approach.Table 2Expert decision Graphical Representation of Ranking.

Analysis for comparison

In this section, the suggested \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} Neutrality Aggregation Operators are compared with the WASPAS approach. Zavadskas42 developed the WASPAS technique to address issues with Multiple Attribute Decision Making (MADM). WASPAS is a crucial instrument for clarifying the directional relationship between an alternative and its ideal counterpart. The weighted sum model (WSM) and weighted product model (WPM) are combined in a novel way in this approach, which is called the weighted aggregated sum product assessment (WASPAS). It is currently widely recognized as a great tool for decision-making due to its mathematical simplicity and ability to produce more accurate findings than WSM and WPM methods.Algorithm

Algorithm

Results of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N_{NA}$$\end{document} WASPAS method.

Discussion

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} compatibility aggregation operators provide the best solution compared to all other techniques because it combines uncertainty, expert hesitation, and various impacts in making a decision. Both the WASPAS method and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}-based approach are providing the same results of ranking; the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} based approach should be more flexible and hence pragmatic, because information about the fuzziness factors along with the neutrality issues are taken into account in this process. Experts’ opinions are integrated in a more versatile manner with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} accumulation compared to numerical weights used under WASPAS; thus, making the whole ranking process more adequate, as it reflects the realities/complexities involved in performance assessment in sports. Furthermore, distinct classes of confidence in expert opinions are accounted for with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} aggregation technique, allowing a more detailed differentiation between alternatives. Even if ambiguity results in two players having similar raw scores, they could rank differently by the level of confidence and neutrality in expert evaluations, rendering this inductive analysis much more insightful than the deterministic approaches represented by WASPAS. The neutrality operator incorporates positive and negative factors in equal proportions, thus preventing the border cases from dominating the decision process and leading to less volatile and more justified rankings. The comparative analysis of player rankings obtained using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} Neutrality Aggregation Operators and the WASPAS method is illustrated in Table 10 and underlines the consistency of the two methods. Figure 3 visually illustrates the ranking order and shows how the results converge, with emphasis on the advantages of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}-based method in handling uncertainty and expert hesitancy. Such comparisons provide considerable evidence that reinforces both the robustness and the reliability of the proposed method, thus endorsing its effectiveness in real-world decision-making scenarios.Table 10Ranking of numerical problem.sr.OperatorsScoring1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWA_{NA}$$\end{document} Operator\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Im (\Psi _{4})\ge \Im (\Psi _{1})\ge \Im (\Psi _{3})\ge \Im (\Psi _{2})$$\end{document}2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWG_{NA}$$\end{document} Operator\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Im (\Psi _{4})\ge \Im (\Psi _{1})\ge \Im (\Psi _{3})\ge \Im (\Psi _{2})$$\end{document}3WASPAS method\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Im (\Psi _{4})\ge \Im (\Psi _{1})\ge \Im (\Psi _{3})\ge \Im (\Psi _{2})$$\end{document}

Sensitivity analysis with respect to weight vector

The identification made by the methods produced identical rankings between the two methods, but the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document}-based method showed greater strength, handled uncertainty better, and adapted to various situations, which made it more appropriate for the complex decision-making process used to evaluate player performance. The combination of expert uncertainty and neutral stance together with unclear driving factors of decision-making provides a solution that meets both mathematical accuracy and practical applicability requirements in sport analytic decision-making. The rankings remain unchanged when weights change because the neutrality-based aggregation operators maintain their functionality through moderate attribute weight changes. The system demonstrates its ability to function effectively in actual decision-making situations because it needs specific weight information for proper operation. Conclusion

The main objective of this investigation focused on creating essential operational principles for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}N$$\end{document} so it can serve as a neutrality aggregation operator for this framework. New aggregation operators such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWA_{NA}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FF\ddot{Z}NWG_{NA}$$\end{document} and were introduced to investigate their essential mathematical properties that included idempotency, monotonicity and boundedness. The operators create more dependable systems for comprehensive information combination when dealing with uncertain decision-making scenarios especially in MAGDM problems. The proposed aggregation operators were tested through their application to genuine soccer player performance assessments. Multiple performance characteristics including passing accuracy, sprint speed, stamina, and defensive contributions were used to perform the evaluation. A comparative analysis with the WASPAS method took place to validate the reliability of our approach because WASPAS stands as a prominent decision-making framework. The experimental results demonstrated that our proposed ranking system extends better flexibility for measuring decision-influences between positive and negative conditions. Aggregation demonstrates superior capabilities to deal with uncertain information combined with imprecise expert assessments. The findings from our case study demonstrate that our method improves decision precision while maintaining stability as well as robustness within unpredictable complex decision-making situations. Due to its ability to involve expert uncertain and subjective information the proposed neutrality aggregation operator becomes a powerful tool for diverse fields from sports analytics into healthcare as well as finance and environmental management applications. The current research will evolve towards merging other methods to improve the decision-making approach. Future research will build upon this project through the integration of different multi-criteria decision-making methods such as TOPSIS, EDAS and ELECTRE for comprehensive approach evaluation. Research will extend the machine learning-based decision system through deep learning methods and predictive analytics to increase real-time performance of proposed approaches.
